CONTENT:
Cool Flames Investigation (CFI) Operational Requirements and Protocols

Operational Requirements:

Droplets must be deployed free floating without any residual momentum in the center of the field of view of the CIR
diagnostics. Diagnostics must be capable of recording the radiant emissions of both hot and cool flames. CIR must
store be able to store digital imagery data from a full day of test point (approx. 8 burns) for later downlink.

Operational Protocols:

Following setup and initialization of the CIR rack and MDCA, the chamber is filled with the appropriate atmosphere for
the day’s test points. Conditions vary in pressure (0.5 atm to 5.0 atm) and oxygen concentration (10 to 30%). A
settling time of approximately 2 minutes elapses prior to initiating the test in order to ensure that the temperature and
pressure of the chamber gases have stabilized. This settling time is followed by the dispensing of a predetermined
amount of fuel (based on the target droplet size) onto a support fiber. When sufficient fuel has been dispensed the
dispensing needles are retracted and a dwell period of at least 10 seconds is allowed for the droplet internal fluid
motion induced by deployment to subside. This is then be followed by initiating power to an igniter pair for a
selectable amount of time ranging from 1 second to 5 seconds after which the igniter will be retracted from the field of
view. A near real-time download of the color camera video is required in order to verify successful droplet
deployment, ignition, and overall progress of the experiment. Pressure and temperature data of the chamber
environment is also required in near real time.

CONTENT:
Cool Flames
 o Accidentally discovered by Sir Humphry Davy in 1810

o Historically cool flames are associated with premixed
   combustion leading to ignition of fuel/air mixtures.
   (commonly encountered in car-engine knock)

o More recently diffusion-controlled, quasi-steady cool flames
   supporting droplet combustion were discovered the FLEX
   team

o Cool-flame low-T chemistry is of importance in new engine
   designs, fuel reforming, etc.

o Important implications with regard to space craft fire safety

CONTENT:
Liñán’s Diffusion Flame Regimes

o In the limit of large activation energies, Linan
                                     showed that structure of diffusion flames
                                     can be captured in terms of 4 regimes
                                       o Frozen-flow
                                       o Partial-burning
                                       o Premixed-flame
                                       o Near-equilibrium diffusion flame

o Since both fuel and oxygen leaks through the
                                     flame partial-burning regime is appropriate
                                     for cool-flame combustion of droplets

Farouk and Dryer (2014)

flame

CONTENT:
Liñán’s Partial Burning Regime: Flame Standoff Ratio

o Farouk and Dryer FSR varies between 2.6 and 3.3. QS theory gives 3.1 in close
      agreement
    o As observed by F&D no dependencies on D0 or CO2 concentration – only O2
      concentration determines FSR
    o Expression is very similar to the classical hot-flame FSR equation
    o Hot flame FSR QS model predicts FSR orders of magnitude greater than
      experimental or numerical results
    o Hot flames for n-alkanes lie in the outer transient-diffusive zone. Cool flames lie
      close to the droplet surface in the convective-diffusive zone where QS theories
      are applicable!
    o Applies to all normal alkanes (T* is very close to each other)
    o Should be useful in planning CFI experiments

Liñán’s Partial Burning Regime: Burning Rate

o   Expression is very similar to the classical
                                      hot-flame burning rate constant equation
                 measured K

o   Turn-over or NTC temperature rather than
                                      adiabatic flame temperature

o   Predicts K within ± 20%

o   Applies to all normal alkanes (T* is very
                                      close to each other)

o   Shows there is a systematic dependence
                                      on initial droplet diameter and pressure

o   Should be useful in planning CFI
                                      experiments

Corrections to the Burning Rate: Oxygen Depletion Effect
o The initial hot flame depletes oxygen in the far field prior to cool flame start
o A correction to the oxygen concentration at the start of the cool flame can be estimated
o Hot flame is a sink for oxygen and convection is small far away from the droplet

Concluding Remarks

o Cool flame burning rates and flame standoff ratios are
      reasonably well predicted using Liñán’s partial burning regime

o Cool flame extinction diameters are correlated against Co2-3/2

o At higher pressures correlation does not work well

o Experiments indicate the pressure effects are not well captured
      by existing reduced mechanisms and needs further study

o Many aspects of the cool flame combustion still remains to be
      studied

0. Nomenclature

CFI             Cool Flame Investigation (ISS droplet combustion experiment focused on cool flames on normal
                alkane fuels)
CIR             Combustion Integrated Rack
FLEX            Flame Extinguishment Experiment (ISS droplet combustion experiments focused on spacecraft fire
                safety)
FLEX-2          Flame Extinguishment Experiment - 2 (ISS droplet combustion experiments focused on fundamental
                aspects)
FOV             filed of view
ISS             International Space Station
MDCA            Multi-used Droplet Combustion Apparatus
NTC             Negative Temperature Coefficient
OH*             hydroxyl radical
TSC             Tele-science Support Center

1. Overview
The CFI experiments are conceived as follow-up experiments to the FLEX and FLEX-2 droplet combustion experiments
onboard the ISS to further explore the newly discovered quasi-steady “cool flames.” Cool flames are controlled by low
temperature chemistry, and occur in the NTC region of n-alkane fuels. FLEX series of experiments showed for the first
time that quasi-steady stable flames can be established surrounding a droplet in microgravity under certain conditions,
without a visible flame at temperatures in the range of 700 to 900 K, unlike the hot flames where the flame temperature
is higher than 1200 K. Since the cool-flame chemistry depends on the chemical structure of the fuel as well as pressure
and temperature, three different fuel combinations were investigated, namely, pure n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-
dodecane mixtures and farnesane ( a renewable jet fuel) at ambient pressures up to 5 atmospheres. Cool flame
extinction occurs when its static stability is lost close to the lower turnover point in the NTC region, addition of helium to
the ambient gas is also considered. Results for droplet burning rates, flame dynamics, droplet extinction diameters, and
flame radiant out put are measured.

2. Introduction (PS):
Understanding the combustion characteristics of liquid hydrocarbon fuels is of practical and fundamental importance.
The United States alone consumes approximately 19 million barrels of liquid fuels per day which averages out to an
astonishing 2.5 gallons per day per person (Reitz, 2013). The primary reasons for such a high reliance on hydrocarbon
fuels as the single most important energy source, not only in the US but globally, is their high energy density, ease of
handling, and portability compared to many other forms of energy. With the emergence of newer internal combustion
engine technologies such as Homogeneous Charge Compression Ignition (HCCI) and Reactivity-Controlled Compression
Ignition (RCCI) engines, understanding the autoignition and heat release chemistry and kinetics of hydrocarbon fuels is
more important that ever. These new technologies coupled with the emergence of major sources of renewable fuels for
both gasoline and diesel applications, including butanols and heavier alcohols, bio-diesel, farnesane and high carbon
number synthetics, further emphasize the need to understand the kinetic effects of blending renewables with
petroleum derived fuels.

a. Objectives
            Cool flame phenomena are influenced by a number of factors, including the fuel molecular structure (for
            pure fuel cases), the ambient environment (pressure, temperature, oxygen and diluent (He) concentrations),
            and the fuel composition (the mixture of fuel structures and perhaps fuel additives such as those used to
            change octane or cetane behavior). Though it is not possible to examine all of these factors in a thorough
            manner, here we propose a set of experiments that explore each one of these factors, namely, fuel structure
            effects, ambient conditions effect, fuel mixture effects, and the presence of fuel additives.

3. Science Overview
        a. Background
            Understanding the combustion characteristics of liquid hydrocarbon fuels is of practical and fundamental
            importance. The United States alone consumes approximately 19 million barrels of liquid fuels per day,
            which averages out to an amazing 2.5 gallons per day per person. The primary reason for such high reliance
            on hydrocarbon fuels as the single most important energy source, not only in the US but in the rest of the
            world, is their high energy density, ease of handling, and portability, compared to many other forms of
            energy sources. With the emergence of newer technologies for internal combustion engines and the
            increasing use of renewable bio-fuels, such as the Homogeneous Charge Compression Ignition (HCCI) engine
            and bio-diesel, that promise cleaner more efficient and sustainable utilization of hydrocarbon fuels, the
            need to understand the fundamental chemical and physical aspects of liquid-fuel combustion is bound to
            increase.

Commercially available transportation fuels, such as gasoline, diesel, and jet fuel, are comprised of many
           hydrocarbon compounds, normal alkanes and branched alkanes being major components. The combustion
           characteristics of these alkanes are quite complex and follow branched-chain reaction pathways involving
           many intermediate radicals. The chemical pathways vary, depending on temperature and, to a lesser extent,
           on pressure. In general it is possible to identify a low-temperature reaction mechanism and a high-
           temperature mechanism, with a transition region between them. Many hydrocarbons exhibit a unique
           feature in this transition region, namely that the reaction rate decreases with increasing temperature,
                                                          3

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: PDF-38ba8297a5 | location: jsonl record 404 | mapping: JSONL domain 'cfi'
CHUNK_ID: PDF-38ba8297a5
DOMAIN: cfi
CATEGORY: document_page
TOPIC: CFI Science Final Report.pdf — page 5
CONTENT_TYPE: source_extraction
EVIDENCE_STATUS: direct_source_text_or_record
SOURCE_FILES: CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf
LOCATOR: {"file": "CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf", "page": 5, "segment": 1}
METADATA: {"page_number": 5, "segment_number": 1, "word_count": 344, "retrieval_guidance": "Use source_files and locator to identify the exact source location. Do not infer missing values.", "source_grounding": "Content is extracted from the uploaded source archive; interpretation should remain within the source's stated scope.", "record_granularity": "PDF page segment", "citation_locator": "PDF page number and segment"}

CONTENT:
leading it to be called the negative-temperature-coefficient (NTC) region. There area a number of models
    available in the literature, simplified chemical-kinetic model involving hundreds of reactions as well as
    detailed mechanisms involving thousands of reactions.

Droplet-combustion experiments (FLEX, FLEX-2) conducted onboard the International Space Station, as part
    of the Flame Extinguishment Experiments, have revealed for the first time that large n-alkane droplets can
    continue to burn quasi-steadily in the NTC region (see, Fig 1). NTC behavior of the heat-release rate is
    necessary for low-temperature quasi-steady droplet burning to be stable. It was observed that the second-
    stage combustion of the droplet controlled by low-temperature NTC chemistry, referred to as ``cool-flame
    combustion" here for the sake of simplicity, abruptly ends at a finite droplet diameter, leaving the unburned
    droplet to vaporize in the hot atmosphere. A vapor cloud was also found to form around the droplet as the
    partially oxidized fuel condensed immediately following cool-flame extinction.

Figure 1. Three representative examples of radiative extinction and subsequent cool flame combustion of n-
    heptane droplets in air. Inset shows the visible flame, backlit droplet images and the vapor cloud formation.

Furthermore, the second-stage flame is shown to lie closer to the droplet surface where the rates of heat
    generation by the NTC chemistry is dynamically balanced by the heat loss rate. Unlike the hot-flame
    combustion where the overall process is controlled by equilibrium thermodynamic properties of the fuel,
    the cool-flame combustion depends on the chemical-kinetic details of the NTC region. These experimental
    and numerical studies provide a new tool to further explore the NTC chemistry of liquid fuels in an entirely
    new way. The experimental cool-flame droplet burning rates provide measures of the heat-release rates
    and stoichiometry, the flame-diameter evolution provides insight into hot-flame diffusive and convective
    heat-loss effects, and the droplet extinction diameter provides a stringent measurement of chemical-kinetic
    characteristic times.

The objectives of the CFI experiments is to further explore the cool flame phenomenon using pure fuels, and
    fuel mixtures over a range of pressures and helium dilution.

CONTENT:
Rationale for microgravity remains the same for all the droplet combustion experiments conducted onboard
           the ISS. In microgravity the droplets burn in a spherically symmetric form, and therefore it is possible to
           model the processes with complex chemical kinetic mechanisms involving thousands of reactions leading to
           a better understanding of the chemistry. Moreover, the cool flames need much longer residence times and
           cannot be observed under normal gravity conditions with droplet flames.
       c. NRA the Investigation was Selected Under
           This project was initiated by NASA through a Science Definition Team (SDT) which formulated the overall
           objectives. It was peer reviewed by an independent panel of scientists and approved by NASA HQ as a
           follow-up experiments to FLEX, and FLEX-2 in the CIR using additional fuels and gases needed to complete
           the test matrix. The experimental hardware is same as that of the FLEX experiments with few minor
           modifications in the data acquisition systems.
       d. Final Experiment Objectives
           Examine the cool flame combustion behavior of n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-dodecane fuel mixtures, and
           farnesane fuels over a rage of pressure from 0.25 atm to 5 atm.
4. Operations
       a. Procedures
           The operational procedure used in the CFI tests is the same as for the FLEX experiments. Once the ISS crew
           installs the required fuel syringe, and the gas bottles containing the gas mixtures in the MDCA and CIR
           facilities, all the actual experimental operations are conducted from the ground at the Telescience Support
           Center (TSC) located at the NASA Glenn Research Center. Prior to each test the combustion chamber is filled
           with the specified gas mixture at the required pressure, and the hot-wire igniters are positioned close to the
           droplet deployment site. Subsequently, a droplet of desired size is dispensed between the hypodermic
           needles and freely deployed, and ignited. The igniter is withdrawn from the FOV once ignition is detected.
           When required a 40 micron silicon-carbide fiber is used to tether the droplet at one place and stop it from
           drifting off from the field of view.

The combustion process is recorded using a backlit, black-and-white camera ( 5 cm X 5 cm FOV) to obtain
            droplet size as a function of time, a UV-sensitive camera is used to image the OH* emission from the flame
            which provides the flame size data. A color camera which is had a live downlink to the TSC is used to
            perform and monitor the overall process. Narrow-band and wide-band radiometers are used to measure
            the radiation emission from the flame. Details of the experimental hardware and the data reduction
            procedures can be found in Dietrich et al. (2014)

b. On-Orbit Testing
           Over two hundred tests were carried out during the CFI experimental campaign. The initial droplet size was
           kept around 4 mm so that radiative extinction and the subsequent cool flame combustion can be observed.
           The list of experiments performed are as follows:

•   Pure n-dodecane at 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 2, 3, and 5 atm.
                •   N-dodecane/Iso-dodecane mixtures: 75/25% and 60/40% by mass at pressures from 0.5 to 5 atm
                •   Helium dilution by 29% and 59% with O2 mole fraction at 0.21
                •   Farnesane: from 0.75 to 3 atm

c. Anomalies That Effect Science Surprises/New Discovery
           No new anomalies or surprises were observed during the CFI experiments. However, as the ambient
           pressure is increased above 2 atm, multiple reignition cycles were observed, where first the hot flame
           extinguishes due to radiative heat loss followed by cool flame, and then reignition to a hot-flame followed
           by a cool flame, with this cycle repeating several times until the fuel droplet is completely consumed. This
           behavior is unexpected based on earlier observations from FLEX experiments. Furthermore, the soot-shell

       d. Organization of Data in PSI
          The organization od the data posted in the PSI website is as follows:

•   Pure n-dodecane data: FLEX-1206 to FLEX-1284
               •   N-dodecane/Iso-dodecane ( 75%-25% & 60%-40%) mixture data: FLEX-1285 to FLEX-1387)
               •   Farnesane data: FLEX-1388 to FLEX-1431

5. Results
       a. Findings
       All the CFI data is yet to be fully analyzed and interpreted using theoretical and numerical models. A brief
       overview of general observations made during preliminary data analysis prior to posting on the PSI website is
       presented below. Interested researchers are welcome to propose to NASA for further analysis and
       interpretation of CFI data.

5.1 Cool flames:
       Cool flames were observed during n-dodecanol combustion as well as n-dodecanol/iso-dodecanol mixture cases.
       The addition of iso-dodecanol reduces the tendency to form cool flames in n-dodecanol. Figure 1 shows a
       typical case of cool-flame behavior for n-dodecanol.

Figure 1. FLEX-1223: N-dodecane at 1.25 atm pressure in air.

5.2 Warm flames:
       Warm flames are a consequence of changing in chemical pathway during n-alkane oxidation. Figure 2 shows
       (Westbrook, 2010) the various chemical pathways: cool flames occur when low-temperature branching is
       dominant, when intermediate temperatures become more active warm flames are established. During warm
       flames the heat release rate dynamically balance the heat loss rate. Addition of helium seems to enhance this
       balance within the NTC region.

LOCATOR: {"file": "CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf", "page": 9, "segment": 1}
METADATA: {"page_number": 9, "segment_number": 1, "word_count": 209, "retrieval_guidance": "Use source_files and locator to identify the exact source location. Do not infer missing values.", "source_grounding": "Content is extracted from the uploaded source archive; interpretation should remain within the source's stated scope.", "record_granularity": "PDF page segment", "citation_locator": "PDF page number and segment"}

CONTENT:
Cool flame extinction is controlled primarily by chemical kinetics. As the droplet size decreases, the residence
time in the reaction zone of the gas-phase diffusion flame is reduced to a value corresponding to which the cool
flame becomes statically unstable, and this leads to cool flame extinction. Figure 4 shows the cool flame
extinction diameter as function of ambient pressure for both free-floated and fiber-supported droplets for pure
n-dodecane burning in air. As mentioned earlier, as the pressure is increased above approximately 2 atm,
reignitions were commonly observed, eventually leading to complete combustion of the fuel droplet. It is
interesting to note that the 40 micron fiber shows relatively little effect on the extinction diameter.

Figure 4. Cool flame extinction diameters as a function of ambient pressure for n-dodecane droplets
                                                 burning in air.

Since the cool flame extinction is an instability phenomenon where the dynamic balance between heat release
and heat loss from the flame is lost at the lower turning point in the NTC region, one would expect addition of
helium to have a substantial influence on the process. Figure 5 illustrates the effect of helium dilution on the
extinction diameter for n-dodecane. Increase helium dilution leads to earlier cool flame extinction giving a larger
extinction diameter.

8

CONTENT:
Figure 5. Effect of helium dilution on cool flame extinction during n-dodecane droplet combustion.

5.4 Sooting behavior as function of ambient pressure:
    The effects and the process of soot formation during cool flame combustion remains largely unexplored. The
    shoot shell that forms during hot flame is stabilized by the balance of force between fluid mechanical drag
    and the thermophoretic force. At low pressures a stable spherical soot-shell is formed. However, as the
    pressure increase the soot-shell becomes diffuse and undefined. The reasons for this phenomenon is still
    not well understood. At low pressures, due to the low flame temperature of the cool flame, soot is not
    formed. The soot that is formed during the hot flame combustion is simply convected away from the droplet
    surface. But, when the pressure increases, residual soot is seen surrounding the droplet even after the hot
    flame extinction. The mechanisms behind this process has not been fully understood, and further analysis
    and research is needed.

9

Figure 6. Backlit images of the droplet during hot and cool flame burning at atmospheric pressures of
           approximately 380, 570, 760, 1520, 2280 and 3800 mmHg. The hot flame images are approximately 4 sec
           after igniter withdraw and the cool flame images are approximately 5 sec after hot flame extinction. The
           droplet in the cool flame image at 1520 mmHg was close to the edge of the FOV. The red circles during hot
           flame burning at the two highest pressures show the approximate location of the droplet surface.

5.5 Summary of findings:
           CFI experiments has provided cool flame burning data for pure n-dodecane, and n-dodecane/iso-dodecane
           mixtures. Farnesane, a commercially available renewable fuel characteristics were also studied. The effect
           of pressure and helium dilution were studied for these fuels. Data for droplet regression rate, hot and cool
           flame diameters, extinction conditions, and radiant emission, both broad band and narrow band, are
           obtained and posted to the PSI website.

b. Benefits
          Low temperature chemistry is of fundamental importance in the combustion of many practical fuels. Low
          temperature chemistry controls the autoignition of fuels when injected into a combustion chamber, and in
          homogeneous mixtures of fuel and oxygen. New internal combustion engines, such as the HCCI engines,
          operate at low temperatures reducing the pollution formation and increasing overall efficiencies. The CFI
          experimental measurements provide a useful way to validate the complex chemical kinetic mechanisms that
          are being developed. The cool flame combustion process itself maybe of use in fuel reformulation
          technologies to produce hydrogen in fuel cell applications.
       c. Patents
          None

       a. 30-Day Operational Accomplishments Report

b. Publications
   The following publications are from the FLEX series of experiments and address various aspects of cool flame
   supported droplet combustion. They are listed here because they form the motivation for the CFI
   experiments. A limited data from CFI experiments pertaining to cool flame extinction diameters are published
   and included in this list.

Dietrich, Daniel L., Vedha Nayagam, Michael C. Hicks, Paul V. Ferkul, Frederick L. Dryer, Tanvir Farouk,
   Benjamin D. Shaw et al. "Droplet combustion experiments aboard the international space
   station." Microgravity Science and Technology 26, no. 2 (2014): 65-76.

Farouk, T., & Dryer, F. L. (2011). Microgravity droplet combustion: effect of tethering fiber on burning rate and
   flame structure. Combustion Theory and Modelling, 15(4), 487-515.

Farouk, Tanvir I., and Frederick L. Dryer. "Isolated n-heptane droplet combustion in microgravity: “Cool
   Flames”–two-stage combustion." Combustion and Flame 161.2 (2014): 565-581.

Farouk, T. I., Hicks, M. C., & Dryer, F. L. (2015). Multistage oscillatory “Cool Flame” behavior for isolated
   alkane droplet combustion in elevated pressure microgravity condition. Proceedings of the Combustion
   Institute, 35(2), 1701-1708.

Farouk, T. I., Dietrich, D., Alam, F. E., & Dryer, F. L. (2017). Isolated n-decane droplet combustion–Dual stage
   and single stage transition to “Cool Flame” droplet burning. Proceedings of the Combustion Institute, 36(2),
   2523-2530.

Farouk, T. I., Dietrich, D., & Dryer, F. L. (2019). Three stage cool flame droplet burning behavior of n-alkane
   droplets at elevated pressure conditions: Hot, warm and cool flame. Proceedings of the Combustion
   Institute, 37(3), 3353-3361.

Liu, Y. C., Xu, Y., Avedisian, C. T., & Hicks, M. C. (2015). The effect of support fibers on micro-convection in
   droplet combustion experiments. Proceedings of the Combustion Institute, 35(2), 1709-1716.

Nayagam, V., Dietrich, D. L., Ferkul, P. V., Hicks, M. C., & Williams, F. A. (2012). Can cool flames support
   quasi-steady alkane droplet burning?. Combustion and Flame, 159(12), 3583-3588.

Nayagam, V., Dietrich, D. L., Hicks, M. C., & Williams, F. A. (2015). Cool-flame extinction during n-alkane
   droplet combustion in microgravity. Combustion and Flame, 162(5), 2140-2147.

Nayagam, V., Dietrich, D.L. and Williams, F.A., 2017. A Burke-Schumann analysis of dual-flame structure
   supported by a burning droplet. International Communications in Heat and Mass Transfer, 87, pp.84-89.

Seshadri, K., Peters, N., Williams, F. A., Nayagam, V., & Paczko, G. (2016). Asymptotic analysis of quasi-
   steady n-heptane droplet combustion supported by cool-flame chemistry. Combustion Theory and
   Modelling, 20(6), 1118-1130.

Shaw, B. D. (2014). ISS droplet combustion experiments-uncertainties in droplet sizes and burning
   rates. Microgravity Science and Technology, 26(2), 89-99.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-001 | location: jsonl record 2131 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'microgravity_combustion'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: COMB-001
DOMAIN: microgravity_combustion
CATEGORY: foundations
TOPIC: Purpose and scope of the knowledge base
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf | Data/Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
This knowledge base describes four related but distinct microgravity combustion research efforts: FLEX (Flame Extinguishment Experiment), FLEX-2 (Fundamental Studies in Droplet Combustion and Flame Extinguishment), CFI (Cool Flames Investigation), and CFI-G (Cool Flames Investigation with Gases). They share the use of the International Space Station (ISS), the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), and controlled combustion experiments, but they do not study identical flame configurations or answer identical scientific questions.

FLEX and FLEX-2 primarily use individual liquid-fuel droplets to investigate burning, flame extinction, flammability limits, suppressant performance, soot, and radiation. CFI extends the droplet-combustion work toward low-temperature, quasi-steady cool-flame behavior using heavier liquid fuels and controlled pressure and diluent conditions. CFI-G studies gaseous-fuel diffusion flames supported by spherical porous burners, including both normal and inverse flame configurations. The distinction between liquid-droplet flames and burner-supported gaseous diffusion flames must be preserved in any retrieval system.

The source collection contains science reports, engineering and science requirements documents, operational protocols, experimental tables, and a multi-sheet CFI data workbook. These document types have different evidentiary roles. Requirements documents describe intended capabilities, test matrices, and success criteria; operational reports describe what was actually performed; science reports summarize observations and interpretations; experimental tables contain individual test records or planned parameter ranges. A model should not treat a planned condition as proof that the condition was tested, nor treat a hypothesis as an established result.

The reports are time-bounded documents. Some explicitly state that data analysis was preliminary or still underway. When answering questions, identify whether a statement is a proposed objective, a hypothesis, an operational fact, a preliminary observation, or a final measured result. If sources disagree, preserve the disagreement and cite the relevant document rather than silently choosing one value.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-002 | location: jsonl record 2132 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'microgravity_combustion'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: COMB-002
DOMAIN: microgravity_combustion
CATEGORY: physical_foundations
TOPIC: Why microgravity is used
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
Microgravity is used because buoyancy-driven flow can distort the spherical symmetry that makes isolated droplet flames and spherical burner flames scientifically useful. In normal gravity, hot combustion products rise and create natural convection. This flow changes the transport of heat, oxygen, fuel vapor, and combustion products around a flame. It can therefore obscure the effects of chemical kinetics, molecular diffusion, radiation, and soot processes that investigators want to isolate.

For droplet combustion, a nearly spherical flame around a freely suspended droplet provides a relatively simple geometry for studying coupled liquid-phase evaporation and gas-phase combustion. In microgravity, the flame and transport fields can approach spherical symmetry for much longer periods than in ordinary gravity. This supports comparisons with models that solve the governing transport and chemical-reaction equations under controlled boundary conditions.

For CFI, the science report states that cool flames require longer residence times and cannot be observed in the same way under normal-gravity droplet-flame conditions. The report explains that microgravity enables spherically symmetric droplet burning and supports the use of complex chemical kinetic mechanisms. The CFI-G report similarly argues that spherical burner-supported diffusion flames are not generally practical in normal gravity. It uses the Richardson number, the ratio of buoyancy to inertial effects, to explain why gravity must be reduced substantially for typical spherical diffusion flames.

The CFI-G report gives an illustrative estimate using a characteristic length of 50 mm, velocity of 5 cm/s, temperature rise of 2000 K, and ambient temperature of 300 K. Under normal gravity, the resulting Richardson number is reported as approximately 1300. The report argues that a microgravity environment is required to reduce buoyancy sufficiently for the intended spherical flame configuration.

Short-duration drop towers are not equivalent to the ISS for these experiments. CFI-G documentation notes that spherical diffusion flames may require approximately 20–30 seconds to approach quasi-steady behavior, while a five-second drop is too short to observe the full development and subsequent cool-flame behavior. The ISS offers longer-duration, low-acceleration conditions, although residual accelerations and disturbances remain relevant experimental considerations.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-008 | location: jsonl record 2138 | mapping: JSONL domain 'CFI'
CHUNK_ID: COMB-008
DOMAIN: CFI
CATEGORY: science_background
TOPIC: Cool flames and negative temperature coefficient chemistry
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CFI/Aspects of Cool-Flame Supported Droplet Combustion in Microgravity_CFI.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
The Cool Flames Investigation (CFI) followed the FLEX and FLEX-2 droplet-combustion experiments to examine quasi-steady cool flames in liquid-fuel droplet combustion. The CFI Science Final Report describes cool flames as being controlled by low-temperature chemical kinetics and associated with the negative temperature coefficient (NTC) region of n-alkane oxidation. In an NTC region, the reaction rate can decrease as temperature increases over a particular temperature interval. This behavior arises from changes in the dominant chemical reaction pathways and intermediate radical chemistry.

The earlier FLEX investigations revealed that large n-alkane droplets can continue to burn quasi-steadily after the visible hot flame has extinguished. The CFI report describes hot flames as having temperatures above approximately 1200 K, whereas the cool-flame regime discussed in the report is associated with temperatures around 700–900 K. These are approximate regime descriptions from the report, not universal thresholds for every fuel and condition.

During a typical two-stage droplet-burning sequence, a hot flame first surrounds the droplet. Radiative heat loss can cause the hot flame to extinguish. Under suitable conditions, a second-stage cool flame can remain near the droplet surface and continue consuming fuel through low-temperature chemistry. The cool flame eventually extinguishes at a finite droplet diameter when its static stability is lost. The report explains this in terms of the balance between heat generation by NTC chemistry and heat loss from the reaction zone.

After cool-flame extinction, the remaining liquid droplet may continue to vaporize in the hot surrounding atmosphere. The CFI report also describes formation of a vapor cloud when partially oxidized fuel condenses after cool-flame extinction. The cool flame therefore differs from an ordinary visible flame and cannot be identified solely from a bright visible image.

The CFI presentation paper describes the discovery of quasi-steady diffusion-controlled cool flames supporting droplet combustion as an important extension of the historically familiar concept of cool flames, which had commonly been associated with premixed ignition and engine knock. For this knowledge base, “cool flame” should be interpreted in context: CFI focuses on low-temperature, diffusion-controlled combustion around liquid droplets.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-009 | location: jsonl record 2139 | mapping: JSONL domain 'CFI'
CHUNK_ID: COMB-009
DOMAIN: CFI
CATEGORY: objectives_and_fuels
TOPIC: CFI research objectives, fuels, and conditions
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
CFI was designed to investigate how cool-flame behavior depends on fuel molecular structure, ambient pressure, oxygen concentration, diluent composition, fuel blending, and possible fuel additives. The science report identifies pure n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-dodecane mixtures, and farnesane as the principal fuel cases. Farnesane is described as a renewable jet-fuel-related compound. The research motivation includes understanding low-temperature oxidation in conventional and emerging engine concepts, including Homogeneous Charge Compression Ignition (HCCI), and understanding how renewable fuel components affect ignition chemistry.

The CFI campaign was conducted aboard the ISS from January through September 2017. The science report states that more than 200 tests were performed. Initial droplet size was generally kept near 4 mm so that hot-flame radiative extinction and subsequent cool-flame combustion could be observed. The listed test program included pure n-dodecane at 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 2, 3, and 5 atm; n-dodecane/iso-dodecane mixtures at 75/25 and 60/40 mass fractions over pressures from 0.5 to 5 atm; helium dilution at 29% and 59% with oxygen mole fraction of 0.21; and farnesane from 0.75 to 3 atm.

The CFI operational protocol describes filling the chamber with the selected gas mixture and pressure, positioning hot-wire igniters, dispensing the selected droplet, and igniting it. The igniter is withdrawn after ignition is detected. A 40-micrometer silicon-carbide fiber may be used when a tether is needed to limit drift and maintain the droplet in the field of view. The protocol document separately specifies operational atmospheric conditions from 0.5 to 5.0 atm and oxygen concentrations of 10–30 percent for daily test-point setup.

The supplied CFI workbook contains multiple sheets, including a Master Sheet, DATA, Camera Settings, Igniter, Comments, and Graphs. The Master Sheet associates test identifiers with dates, CIR identifiers, pressure, oxygen, nitrogen, helium, fiber use, and fuel. The DATA sheet includes initial diameter, hot- and cool-flame extinction diameters, hot- and cool-flame burning-rate constants, pressure, reignitions, burn time, radiometer-derived quantities, and analysis status. These workbook fields should be interpreted using their exact column labels and units.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-010 | location: jsonl record 2140 | mapping: JSONL domain 'CFI'
CHUNK_ID: COMB-010
DOMAIN: CFI
CATEGORY: diagnostics_and_procedure
TOPIC: CFI operational sequence and diagnostics
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Operational Requirements and Protocols.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
The CFI Operational Requirements and Protocols document specifies that droplets must be deployed as free-floating droplets without residual momentum in the center of the CIR diagnostic field of view. Diagnostics must be capable of recording radiant emissions from both hot and cool flames. The CIR must be able to store digital imagery from a full day of test points, described as approximately eight burns, for later downlink.

The documented operational sequence begins with installation and initialization of the CIR rack and MDCA. The chamber is filled with the appropriate atmosphere for the day's test points. The protocol specifies pressure conditions from 0.5 to 5.0 atm and oxygen concentrations from 10 to 30 percent. A settling interval of approximately two minutes is allowed for chamber gas temperature and pressure to stabilize. A predetermined amount of fuel is then dispensed onto a support fiber. After sufficient fuel is dispensed, the needles are retracted and a dwell period of at least ten seconds is allowed for deployment-induced internal liquid motion to subside. An igniter pair is powered for a selectable interval of one to five seconds and then retracted from the field of view.

Near-real-time color-camera video is required to verify droplet deployment, ignition, and experiment progress. Chamber pressure and temperature are also required in near real time. These operational data are important for identifying whether a test proceeded as intended and for interpreting any anomalous behavior.

The CFI Science Final Report describes a backlit black-and-white camera with a 5 cm by 5 cm field of view to measure droplet size over time. A UV-sensitive camera images OH* emission and provides flame-size information. A color camera supplies a live downlink to the Telescience Support Center for monitoring. Narrow-band and wide-band radiometers measure flame radiation. The science report also describes the optional use of a 40-micrometer silicon-carbide support fiber.

The different diagnostics measure different physical quantities. Backlit imaging tracks liquid-droplet geometry; OH* imaging tracks flame emission; color imaging gives overall visual context; radiometers quantify emitted radiation. Their measurements should be synchronized and interpreted together rather than treated as interchangeable indicators of flame presence or extinction.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-011 | location: jsonl record 2141 | mapping: JSONL domain 'CFI'
CHUNK_ID: COMB-011
DOMAIN: CFI
CATEGORY: observations
TOPIC: CFI preliminary findings: cool flames, warm flames, extinction, soot
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
The CFI Science Final Report explicitly characterizes its findings as a brief overview of general observations made during preliminary data analysis. It states that the complete dataset had not yet been fully analyzed and interpreted using theoretical and numerical models. Consequently, these findings should be described as reported preliminary observations rather than as a complete final theory.

The report states that cool flames were observed during n-dodecane combustion and in n-dodecane/iso-dodecane mixture cases. It reports that adding iso-dodecane reduces the tendency of n-dodecane to form cool flames. The report also discusses warm flames as a consequence of changes in the chemical pathways during n-alkane oxidation. Warm flames were typically observed at higher pressures after radiative extinction; the flame may not be visible in the color camera, after which a hot flame can reignite and quickly extinguish into a cool flame.

Cool-flame extinction is described as primarily controlled by chemical kinetics. As droplet size decreases, residence time in the gas-phase reaction zone decreases. When the residence time reaches a value at which the cool flame becomes statically unstable, cool-flame extinction occurs. The report associates extinction with loss of the dynamic balance between heat release and heat loss near the lower turning point in the NTC region.

Above approximately 2 atm, multiple reignition cycles were commonly observed. A hot flame could extinguish through radiative heat loss, a cool flame could follow, and a hot flame could reignite; the sequence could repeat until the droplet was consumed. The report describes this behavior as unexpected relative to earlier FLEX observations.

The report states that increasing helium dilution leads to earlier cool-flame extinction, represented by a larger extinction diameter. It also reports pressure-dependent soot-shell behavior: at lower pressures a stable spherical soot shell is observed, whereas at higher pressures the shell becomes diffuse and poorly defined. Residual soot may remain around the droplet after hot-flame extinction at higher pressures. The mechanisms behind these soot observations were not fully understood in the report and require further analysis.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-012 | location: jsonl record 2142 | mapping: JSONL domain 'CFI'
CHUNK_ID: COMB-012
DOMAIN: CFI
CATEGORY: applications
TOPIC: Why CFI matters for engines and fuel chemistry
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
CFI measurements are relevant to engine research because low-temperature oxidation chemistry affects fuel autoignition and heat release. The CFI Science Final Report identifies Homogeneous Charge Compression Ignition (HCCI) as an example of an engine concept that operates through low-temperature combustion processes. Better understanding of low-temperature chemistry can support engine designs that seek improved efficiency and reduced pollutant formation.

Commercial transportation fuels are mixtures of many hydrocarbon compounds, including normal and branched alkanes. Their oxidation proceeds through complex branched-chain reaction pathways involving many intermediate radicals. The dominant pathways depend on temperature and, to a lesser extent, pressure. The NTC region is particularly important because the reaction rate may decrease with increasing temperature over part of the transition between low- and high-temperature chemistry.

Fuel blending can alter the low-temperature chemical response. CFI therefore examines pure n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-dodecane blends, and farnesane. The report frames these experiments as a way to investigate fuel molecular structure, mixture composition, ambient conditions, and potential additive effects. The CFI data can be used to test detailed chemical-kinetic mechanisms and improve models that predict ignition and combustion behavior.

The science report also notes a possible connection between cool-flame combustion and fuel-reformulation technologies for producing hydrogen for fuel-cell applications. This is presented as a potential benefit, not as a demonstrated outcome of the CFI flight experiment.

A RAG system should answer application questions with calibrated language. It may state that CFI provides measurements useful for validating low-temperature kinetic mechanisms and informing HCCI-related combustion research. It should not claim that CFI itself developed a commercial engine, directly reduced emissions, or demonstrated a hydrogen-production system.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-016 | location: jsonl record 2146 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'cross_investigation'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: COMB-016
DOMAIN: cross_investigation
CATEGORY: comparison
TOPIC: How FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G differ
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf | Data/Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G form a related research sequence, but their experimental systems and scientific emphasis differ.

FLEX investigates the extinction of burning liquid droplets and the efficacy of gaseous fire suppressants. Its principal outcomes include flammability boundaries, limiting oxygen index, extinction diameter, burning histories, radiation, soot behavior, and model-validation data.

FLEX-2 extends fundamental droplet-combustion and extinction studies. Its science requirements include extinction boundaries, soot and radiation measurements, fuel comparisons and mixtures, and controlled droplet translation. Its supplied test-matrix file lists fuels and ranges of droplet diameter, oxygen concentration, pressure, and flow velocity.

CFI follows the FLEX investigations to focus on low-temperature cool-flame combustion around liquid droplets. It investigates n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-dodecane mixtures, and farnesane, with variations in pressure and helium dilution. Its key measurements include droplet regression, hot- and cool-flame diameters, extinction behavior, and radiant emission.

CFI-G shifts from liquid droplets to gaseous fuels supplied through spherical porous burners. It studies normal and inverse cool diffusion flames, fuel and oxidizer flow conditions, dilution, pressure, and flame transitions. Its central modeling emphasis is CFD and cool-flame chemical kinetics for spherical gaseous diffusion flames.

The common scientific themes are microgravity transport, spherical flame structure, extinction, low-temperature chemistry, radiation, soot, experimental diagnostics, and validation of predictive models. The central difference is the physical configuration: single liquid droplets in FLEX/FLEX-2/CFI versus burner-supported gaseous diffusion flames in CFI-G. A language model should not transfer a numerical range, fuel list, mechanism, or observation from one investigation to another unless the source explicitly supports that transfer.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-017 | location: jsonl record 2147 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'data_engineering'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: COMB-017
DOMAIN: data_engineering
CATEGORY: rag_guidance
TOPIC: How to interpret the supplied experimental files safely
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/Flex/PSI-69_Experimental table_FLEX.csv | Data/Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv | Data/CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Normal Flames.csv | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Inverse Flames.csv
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
The experimental files have different structures and should be indexed with investigation-specific metadata. The FLEX CSV is a test-level dataset with one row per test and fields describing fuel, date/time, pressure, ambient gas composition, droplet size, visible flame extinction diameter, burning rate, burn time, and test end. The FLEX-2 CSV supplied here is a test-matrix table of fuels and parameter ranges, not a row-by-row record of completed tests. The CFI workbook is a multi-sheet dataset with test metadata, measured analysis fields, camera settings, igniter settings, and qualitative comments. The CFI-G normal and inverse CSVs are separate test-level tables with different columns.

Recommended metadata for each indexed chunk or row include: investigation name; experiment configuration; source filename; source document type; page, sheet, or row identifier; test identifier where available; fuel; pressure and unit; oxygen mole fraction; diluent or suppressant; droplet diameter or burner flow condition; flame configuration; and whether the content is a requirement, planned matrix, observation, or interpreted finding.

Do not convert units silently. For example, the FLEX table uses pressure in mmHg, the CFI-G tables use approximate pressure in bar, and the CFI workbook uses atm. Store the original value and unit, and add a normalized value only as a separate, explicitly computed field. Preserve the source column name as well as any normalized schema name.

Missing values must remain null or blank. Do not replace them with zero, “false,” or an inferred result. Qualitative comments should be retained because they may explain why a test was unsuccessful or why a measurement should be excluded. Test-end labels and abbreviations should be preserved verbatim until a source-backed definition is available.

For retrieval quality, keep individual test records small and atomic, but include enough surrounding metadata to make each record interpretable. For explanatory narrative chunks, use one coherent topic per chunk and repeat essential context such as the investigation name. This reduces the risk that a retrieved passage about a droplet flame will be mistaken for a CFI-G burner flame.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: COMB-018 | location: jsonl record 2148 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'source_quality'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: COMB-018
DOMAIN: source_quality
CATEGORY: limitations_and_discrepancies
TOPIC: Source limitations and unresolved inconsistencies
CONTENT_TYPE: explanatory_synthesis
EVIDENCE_STATUS: source_grounded_synthesis_from_uploaded_material
SOURCE_FILES: Data/CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI Operational Requirements and Protocols.pdf | Data/CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx | Data/CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf
LAYER: conceptual_explanation
OTHER_FIELDS: {"version": "1.0"}

CONTENT:
Several limitations should be explicitly represented in the knowledge base. First, the CFI Science Final Report says that its findings are preliminary and that the complete dataset had not yet been fully analyzed and interpreted with theoretical and numerical models. Its observations should therefore not be presented as exhaustive final conclusions.

Second, the CFI sources describe overlapping but not identical operating ranges. The science report describes experimental pressures for pure n-dodecane extending from 0.5 to 5 atm and other fuel cases over specified subsets of that range. The operational protocol gives a general chamber range of 0.5–5.0 atm and oxygen concentration of 10–30 percent. Other overview material may describe different subsets or objectives. These values should be retained with their source context rather than compressed into a single universal range.

Third, the CFI project description supplied in earlier background notes included a possible pressure-range discrepancy between an objective-level statement and an operational range. The uploaded CFI Science Final Report and operational protocol should be used as the primary source references, and any conflicting value should be recorded with its exact document and section before being reconciled.

Fourth, the CFI-G Final Report is dated 21 November 2022 and explicitly states that data analysis and publication were still underway and that an update was expected. Treat its conclusions as report-date findings, not necessarily the final state of the research.

Fifth, the CFI-G proposal hypothesized use of n-pentane, but the final report states that the hardware configuration could not support n-pentane testing because of its low vapor pressure and that ethane was used as an alternate. A model should distinguish proposed fuels from actual substitutions.

Finally, the supplied ZIP contains documents with overlapping titles and versions, including multiple FLEX engineering documents and two copies of the FLEX-2 Science Requirements Document (one labeled “No Hypertext”). Their content should be compared before deduplication. Similar filenames do not prove that files are byte-identical or semantically identical.

>>> source: RAG/CompleteTamerRAGChunks/microgravity_combustion_complete_rag.jsonl | chunk: GUIDE-RAG-001 | location: jsonl record 2149 | mapping: corpus-wide chunk (domain 'microgravity_combustion'), shared by the corpus's four investigations | SHARED with: PSI-68 (FLEX-2), PSI-69 (FLEX), PSI-159 (ACME CFI-G)
CHUNK_ID: GUIDE-RAG-001
DOMAIN: microgravity_combustion
CATEGORY: corpus_guide
TOPIC: Corpus scope, source hierarchy, and interpretation rules
CONTENT_TYPE: rag_instructional_context
EVIDENCE_STATUS: curation_guidance
SOURCE_FILES: Data.zip
LAYER: corpus_navigation
LOCATOR: {"file": "Data.zip", "scope": "entire archive"}
METADATA: {"investigations": ["FLEX", "FLEX-2", "CFI", "CFI-G"], "source_types": ["PDF reports", "CSV experimental tables", "XLSX workbook", "embedded metadata ZIP archives"], "recommended_retrieval_filters": ["domain", "category", "source_files", "locator.file", "locator.page", "locator.row", "locator.test_id"], "caution": "Do not conflate planned test matrices, engineering requirements, operational procedures, preliminary observations, and measured records."}

CONTENT:
This corpus is based on the uploaded Data.zip archive. It covers four distinct investigations: FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G. FLEX, FLEX-2, and CFI concern liquid-fuel droplet combustion, while CFI-G concerns gaseous-fuel diffusion flames on spherical porous burners. The corpus contains source PDF text segmented by page, CSV rows retained as individual experimental records, CFI workbook records grouped by test identifier across sheets, metadata documents extracted from embedded ZIP archives, dataset inventories, and a separate conceptual synthesis layer.

Evidence categories must remain distinct. Engineering requirements describe intended capabilities. Science requirements and test matrices describe objectives or planned conditions. Operational protocols describe procedures. Experimental tables record test-specific conditions and outcomes. Science reports may describe observations, interpretations, or hypotheses, and some explicitly characterize their analysis as preliminary. Never turn a planned test into a completed test, a hypothesis into a finding, or a blank data field into a negative result.

When answering a scientific question, retrieve the relevant investigation and source document first. For test-specific questions, prefer experimental_record chunks and cite their test identifier and row or workbook location. For methods or objectives, use the relevant requirements or protocol PDF and cite its page. For scientific interpretation, use science reports and retain the report's own qualification and uncertainty. If sources disagree, report the discrepancy with both source locations rather than silently reconciling it. Preserve original units and column names; any unit conversion should be separately labeled and reproducible.

The conceptual synthesis chunks are useful for orientation but are secondary to direct source extraction. If a synthesis statement conflicts with a primary source passage or data record, the primary source should control and the conflict should be surfaced.
